Wait, but actually Array.IndexGet returns `UndefinedValue` for out-of-range, which is non-nil. So the VM OpIndex won't distinguish. Good enough.

Now, how to implement default check in bytecode:
- Load source, index, result on stack.
- We need to conditionally evaluate default expression if result is undefined.
- We can use OpJumpFalsy: Undefined.IsFalsy() is true (let me verify). Then:
  ```
  ... [result]
  OpJumpFalsy  -> jumps if falsy (undefined), popping result
  ; if not undefined: value is result, skip default
  ```
  But OpJumpFalsy pops the value when jumping, and doesn't pop when not jumping. So:
  ```
  OpJumpFalsy defaultLabel
  ; result remains on stack
  OpJump afterDefault
  defaultLabel:
  <compile default>
  afterDefault:
  OpDefineLocal idx
  ```
  Wait, OpJumpFalsy: it does `v.sp--` then checks `v.stack[v.sp].IsFalsy()`. So on jump, it pops. On not-jump, it doesn't pop (sp already decremented?
Let me re-read:
  ```
  case parser.OpJumpFalsy:
      v.ip += 4
      v.sp--
      if v.stack[v.sp].IsFalsy() {
          pos := ...
          v.ip = pos - 1
      }
  ```
  It always decrements sp by 1. On jump, the value is popped. On not-jump, the value is still on stack (at old sp-1 but now sp points past it).
Actually after `v.sp--`, the value remains at `v.stack[v.sp]`, and sp points to that slot (since sp was decremented, the value is now at top). So on
not-jump, value is at top of stack (sp index). On jump, value is popped (gone). Good.

  So:
  ```
  ... push value
  OpJumpFalsy L1
  OpJump L2
  L1:
  ... compile default
  L2:
  OpDefineLocal idx
  ```
  This works. But note if value is undefined and default is evaluated, then stack has default value. If value is not undefined, stack has value. Good.

  For simple binding without default, just OpDefineLocal idx directly after indexing.

Now, how to compile source and keep it while extracting multiple elements? We need to store source in a temporary local. Since the compiler manages a
symbol table, we can define a temp symbol like `:destructure` (similar to iterator `:it`). Then:
```
compile source
OpDefineLocal temp
for each element:
    OpGetLocal temp
    push index constant
    OpIndex
    [default logic]
    OpDefineLocal var
```
This duplicates the source reference (arrays/maps are reference objects, so no deep copy issue). The source is immutable? Tengo values are immutable
except via set operations. Storing the source in a temp local is fine.

But wait, OpDefineLocal temp consumes the source from stack and stores it. Then each OpGetLocal loads it. Good.

For nested patterns, e.g., `[a, [b, c]] := src`:
```
compile src
DEFL temp
GETL temp ; 0
CONST 0
INDEX
DEFL a
GETL temp ; 1
CONST 1
INDEX
; now we have subarray on stack; need to store it in another temp
DEFL temp2
GETL temp2 ; 0
CONST 0
INDEX
DEFL b
GETL temp2 ; 1
CONST 1
INDEX
DEFL c
```
We can either reuse a single temp variable for nested levels (careful: while evaluating defaults that reference outer variables, the temp must not be
overwritten before use). Since we process depth-first and each sub-pattern's source is consumed before moving on, we could reuse one temp? But
defaults may reference bindings established earlier, and those are separate variables, not the temp. The temp can be reused as long as nested
processing is complete before we use it again. But if we have `[a, [b, c], d] := src`, after extracting subarray at index 1 into temp, we process b
and c; then we need d from src (outer temp). So we need to keep outer temp until all elements are done. We could use a stack of temps, or use a unique
temp per nesting level (compiler can define fresh temps). Simplest: define a new temp local per nested pattern invocation (or per level). Since the
compiler's symbol table is a stack, we can define temp locals in the same scope; they'll just consume extra local slots. That's fine.

Actually, we could avoid temps entirely by duplicating the source with a new opcode, but adding opcodes is more invasive. Temp locals are simplest.

### Compiling function parameters

Function params with patterns: `func([a, b], {x: c}) { ... }`. When the function is called, args are pushed on stack. The VM's OpCall sets up frame:
`basePointer = sp - numArgs`, and locals initialized? Let's look at how OpCall handles NumLocals and how params map to locals.

In OpCall:
```
v.curFrame.basePointer = v.sp - numArgs
...
v.sp = v.sp - numArgs + callee.NumLocals
```
The arguments are placed at stack[basePointer..basePointer+numArgs-1]. Locals beyond numArgs are zero (nil). The compiler defines params via
`symbolTable.Define(p.Name)` and marks `LocalAssigned=true`, and does NOT emit OpDefineLocal for params; it relies on args being already at those
local slots. For a destructuring param like `[a,b]`, the function receives one argument (the array), but needs to bind `a` and `b` as locals. So in
the function prologue, we need to compile destructuring code that reads from the arg slot and defines `a`,`b`.

But there's a subtlety: the parameter itself — should the pattern bind to a variable? In JS, `function([a,b]) {}` — `a` and `b` are bound, no `arg`
variable. So the pattern param occupies one arg slot (the source array), but it's not itself a named variable. We need to:
- Define symbols for each pattern leaf.
- Mark the pattern's source local (the argument slot) as... hmm. The arg slot is used by the pattern as source. We need a temp to hold the source? The
source is already at the arg's local slot. But that slot corresponds to a symbol if we define one. We can define a temp symbol for the pattern
parameter (e.g., `:param0`) at that arg index, marked LocalAssigned=true, so the arg lands there. Then compile destructuring using that temp, defining
the leaf symbols as locals after the existing params.

Wait, but `NumParameters` is used by the VM to determine how many args to accept. If `func([a,b])` has 1 parameter, NumParameters=1. The VM's OpCall
uses numArgs (actual args) and compiled function's NumParameters in `checkArguments`? Let me look at how arguments are checked.

func equalFuncType(t *testing.T, expected, actual *FuncType) {
	require.Equal(t, expected.Params.LParen, actual.Params.LParen)
	require.Equal(t, expected.Params.RParen, actual.Params.RParen)
	equalIdents(t, expected.Params.List, actual.Params.List)
}

func equalIdents(t *testing.T, expected, actual []*Ident) {
	require.Equal(t, len(expected), len(actual))
	for i := 0; i < len(expected); i++ {
		equalExpr(t, expected[i], actual[i])
	}
}

func equalExprs(t *testing.T, expected, actual []Expr) {
	require.Equal(t, len(expected), len(actual))
	for i := 0; i < len(expected); i++ {
		equalExpr(t, expected[i], actual[i])

func TestIdentListString(t *testing.T) {
	identListVar := &parser.IdentList{
		List: []*parser.Ident{
			{Name: "a"},
			{Name: "b"},
			{Name: "c"},
		},
		VarArgs: true,
	}

	expectedVar := "(a, b, ...c)"
	if str := identListVar.String(); str != expectedVar {
		t.Fatalf("expected string of %#v to be %s, got %s",
			identListVar, expectedVar, str)
	}

	identList := &parser.IdentList{
		List: []*parser.Ident{
			{Name: "a"},
			{Name: "b"},
			{Name: "c"},
		},
		VarArgs: false,
	}

	expected := "(a, b, c)"
	if str := identList.String(); str != expected {
		t.Fatalf("expected string of %#v to be %s, got %s",
			identList, expected, str)
	}
}

}

// writePadding generates n bytes of padding.
func (f *formatter) writePadding(n int) {
	if n <= 0 { // No padding bytes needed.
		return
	}
	buf := *f.buf
	oldLen := len(buf)
	newLen := oldLen + n

	if newLen > MaxStringLen {
		panic(ErrStringLimit)
	}

	// Make enough room for padding.
	if newLen > cap(buf) {
		buf = make(fmtbuf, cap(buf)*2+n)
		copy(buf, *f.buf)
	}
	// Decide which byte the padding should be filled with.

	if f.zero {
		padByte = byte('0')

### Parser changes

I'll add pattern AST nodes in a new file `parser/pattern.go` (or add to expr.go/ast.go).

Nodes:

```go
type ArrayPattern struct {
    LBrack   Pos
    Elements []Expr
    RBrack   Pos
}

func (e *ArrayPattern) exprNode() {}
func (e *ArrayPattern) Pos() Pos { return e.LBrack }
func (e *ArrayPattern) End() Pos { return e.RBrack + 1 }
func (e *ArrayPattern) String() string { ... }

type MapPattern struct {
    LBrace   Pos
    Fields   []*MapPatternField
    RBrace   Pos
}

type MapPatternField struct {
    Key    string
    KeyPos Pos
    Value  Expr // pattern; nil for shorthand
}

type DefaultExpr struct {
    Expr   Expr
    Default Expr
    TokenPos Pos
}

type RestExpr struct {
    Expr      Expr // must be Ident
    EllipsisPos Pos
}
```

Should these implement Expr? They'll be used as LHS expressions in AssignStmt and as params. Yes, make them implement Expr (exprNode). They aren't
valid general expressions, but that's okay since they only appear in pattern contexts.

Alternatively, define a separate `Pattern` interface. But to keep things simple, implement `Expr` and only allow in pattern contexts.

`parseExprList` is called only in parseSimpleStmt for LHS and RHS. We can add a separate `parsePatternList` for LHS.

But there's the issue that a statement could be `[a, b] := src` or `[a, b]` (expression) or `[a, b] = x` (invalid destructuring with =) or `[a, b] +
x` (binary expression statement). To handle all, we need to parse `[a, b]` at statement start as either pattern or literal. The cleanest is to parse
it as a pattern-or-literal in one pass, then convert.

Let me design a `parsePatternExpr()` that parses array/map patterns. When called at the start of `parseExprListPattern`, if the token is `[` or `{`,
parse as pattern. But we also need to handle identifiers and other expressions (for simple LHS like `a`). So `parsePatternExpr` should:
- If token is `LBrack` -> parseArrayPattern
- If token is `LBrace` -> parseMapPattern
- Else -> parseExpr() (normal expression; but if it's followed by `=` default? e.g. simple LHS `a` in `a = 5` is just normal expr; defaults only
inside patterns).

Wait, but `[a, b]` in a statement like `[a, b] := src` — if we call `parsePatternExpr` on `[`, it parses array pattern. If the next token after
pattern is `:=`, good. If it's `+ x` (binary expression), then we have ArrayPattern + BinaryExpr? The binary parser expects LHS Expr and continues.
ArrayPattern implements Expr, so `parseBinaryExpr` could handle it. But we'd need to convert ArrayPattern to ArrayLit for it to compile as an
expression. Since pattern ASTs aren't compiled as regular expressions, we'd need a conversion step.

Simpler approach: parse `[a,b]` as an ArrayPattern always when at LHS. Then:
- If after parsing the full pattern list we see `:=` or `=`, create AssignStmt with pattern LHS.
- Otherwise, if we encounter a binary operator after an ArrayPattern/MapPattern, we need to convert pattern to literal before continuing the binary
expression parse. But `parseExprListPattern` doesn't do binary parsing; it just parses a list of comma-separated primary expressions. Actually
`parseSimpleStmt` calls `parseExprList` which calls `parseExpr` -> `parseBinaryExpr` -> `parseUnaryExpr` -> parseOperand. So the whole expression
including binary is parsed in one call.

Let me restructure: In `parseSimpleStmt`, instead of `p.parseExprList()`, do:
```go
x := p.parseExprListWithPattern()
```
where `parseExprListWithPattern` parses each expression with `parseExprWithPattern`:
- `parseExprWithPattern` if token is `[`/`{` -> parse pattern (with full binary? no, patterns don't have binary ops outside).
- else -> `parseExpr()`.

Then after we get the LHS list, if the first is a pattern and the token is `:=` or `=`, good. If it's a pattern and token is not assignment, then it
was actually an expression (literal) and we should convert the pattern AST to a literal AST before proceeding with further binary operators... but the
binary operators haven't been consumed yet. E.g., `[a,b] + c` — our pattern parser would parse `[a,b]` and stop at `+`. Then
`parseExprListWithPattern` returns `[ArrayPattern]` with current token `+`. `parseSimpleStmt` sees token `+` not in switch, and would return
ExprStmt{ArrayPattern}, leaving `+ c` unconsumed -> parse error. To avoid this, we need to continue binary parsing after converting pattern to
literal.

Alternative: After seeing the pattern and the next token is not assignment, we can convert the ArrayPattern to ArrayLit and then continue parsing
binary operators by calling the rest of `parseBinaryExpr` logic. This is doable: implement `parseExprWithPattern` as:
```go
if p.token == LBrack || p.token == LBrace {
    pat := p.parsePattern()
    if isAssignmentToken(p.token) {
        return pat
    }
    // convert to literal and continue as expression
    lit := convertPatternToLit(pat)
    return p.parseBinaryExprContinue(lit, ...)  // hmm need to continue from unary
}
return p.parseExpr()
```

But `parseBinaryExpr` needs precedence and loops. We can call `p.parseBinaryExprFrom(lit, token.LowestPrec+1)`? Let me look at parseBinaryExpr
structure: `parseBinaryExpr(prec1)` calls `parseUnaryExpr()` first, then loops. We could add `parseBinaryExprTail(x, prec1)` that does the loop. But
pattern + binary might be rare. Let's support it to not break existing code: `[a,b] + x` should still work. However, is `[a,b] + x` valid currently?
Let's test.

REASONING_CONTENT
So `[a,b] + [1,2]` is valid currently (array concatenation). We must not break that.

So I'll implement a pattern parser that:
1. Parses a pattern if token is `[` or `{`.
2. If the next token is `:=`, `=`, or `,` (within a list? no, after a pattern in a list LHS can't have more), or `in` (for for-in? no), then it's a
pattern.
3. Otherwise, convert to a literal and continue parsing as an expression.

Actually, the decision can be deferred: parse as pattern, then if next token is not `:=`/`=` (assignment), convert to literal. But we also need to
handle `[a,b] := src` vs `[a,b] = src`. For `=` we keep pattern and let compiler error "cannot use destructuring with =". For other tokens, convert.

But what about `[a, b] in seq` in a for-in loop: `for [a,b] in seq` — should that be a pattern? Task doesn't mention for-in destructuring. Current
grammar `parseSimpleStmt(true)` handles `x in y`. If we parse `[a,b]` as pattern and token is `in`, then `case token.In` would see a pattern. It would
then try to interpret x[0] as Ident and error "expected identifier". That's acceptable (probably a compile error anyway). But should we convert to
literal for `in`? For `for [a,b] in seq`, current code parses `[a,b]` as ArrayLit and then errors in the for-in handler because x[0] isn't an Ident.
Actually it calls `p.errorExpected` and creates a `_` ident. So it silently produces `for _ in seq`. With our pattern, it would similarly error. Fine.
But to be safe, for `in` token we can keep as pattern (it'll be an error or maybe we could support destructuring in for-in? Not required).

Now, the pattern parser must support:
- `[a, b]` - array pattern
- `[a, ...rest]` - rest (must be last)
- `[a, [b, c]]` - nested
- `[a, {x: b}]` - nested map
- `[a = 1, b]` - defaults
- `[]`, `{}` - empty
- `{x}` shorthand
- `{x: a}` rename
- `{x: a = 50}` rename + default
- `{x = 50}` shorthand + default? Is that valid? "including shorthand `{x}` and renaming `{x: a}` (with optional defaults like `{x: a = 50}`)". It
doesn't explicitly mention `{x = 50}` but it would be natural. I'll support it.

Inside patterns, the "elements" are patterns: identifiers, nested arrays/maps, defaults wrapping a pattern. Also possibly `_`? The parser's
`parseIdent` defaults to `_` if token isn't Ident, but that's for error recovery.

Rest: only in array patterns, last element. The parser should enforce "rest element must be last" at parse time? The task says compile-time errors
must include the substring. Parser errors are also compile-time errors. We can enforce in parser (simpler) and the error message includes "rest
element must be last". Let's enforce in parser when parsing array pattern: if we see `...`, parse rest expr, then require `]` (or maybe allow comma
then error). If there's more after rest, emit error "rest element must be last".

But also `cannot use destructuring with =`: this is a compile-time error when a pattern is on LHS of `=`. We can enforce in compiler (which is where
`=` vs `:=` is known) — compiler error message "cannot use destructuring with =". Or parser can detect. The parser knows the token is `=` when it
creates AssignStmt. But parser errors are also fine. Let me check the requirement: "Compile-time errors must include these substrings". Either parser
or compiler error works as long as the error string contains the substring. I'll put "cannot use destructuring with =" in the compiler (or parser).
Parser is simpler: when `parseSimpleStmt` sees pattern LHS and token is `Assign`, emit error "cannot use destructuring with =". But then we still need
to parse RHS and recover. Let's do parser error. Actually, wait: what about `[a] = x` where `=` is just a regular assignment to an array? There's no
valid interpretation of `[a] = x` in Tengo (you can't assign to array literal). So erroring is fine.

But there's a subtlety: `parseSimpleStmt` is also used in for headers with `parseSimpleStmt(false)`. The error should still work.

Now let's also think about how to handle pattern elements that are invalid, e.g., `[1, 2] := src` — array pattern with IntLit elements. We should emit
a compile-time error. In parser, when parsing array pattern, we can restrict elements to pattern forms (Ident, nested Array/Map pattern, DefaultExpr).
If we see an IntLit etc., emit error "invalid pattern element" or something. The task doesn't specify this error string, so any reasonable error is
fine. But we need to ensure `[a,b]` parsed as pattern only in LHS, and `[1,2] := x` errors. Let's make parsePattern strictly parse pattern forms:
- An element can be:
  - `...` followed by Ident -> RestExpr
  - Ident -> Ident
  - `[` -> nested ArrayPattern
  - `{` -> nested MapPattern
  - default: `element = expr` -> DefaultExpr wrapping element (only if element is a valid pattern). For shorthand map `{x = 1}`, element is Ident then
`=`.
- Otherwise error "invalid pattern element".

But careful: `[a]` at statement start that's actually an expression statement — `a` inside `[...]` is a valid pattern (ident). So `[a]` as an
expression would be converted to ArrayLit with element `a`. Good. `[a + b]` — `a + b` is not a valid pattern element (it's a BinaryExpr). But as an
expression statement, `[a+b]` is valid. So our strict pattern parser would reject it! That's a problem.

So the pattern parser can't be too strict if it must also serve as a literal parser for non-assignment contexts. We need a way to distinguish.
Options:
1. Parse pattern elements more loosely (as arbitrary expressions with optional `= default`), then when converting to literal, use the expression. But
`a = 1` as a default inside a pattern is syntactically distinct from `a + b`; we can parse `a = 1` as a DefaultExpr. For `a + b`, parse as BinaryExpr.
So the parser can accept either pattern forms or general expressions. Then:
   - If followed by assignment, it's a pattern; if an element is a general expression (not a valid pattern), the compiler errors.
   - If not followed by assignment, convert to literal; if an element is a DefaultExpr/RestExpr, that's an invalid literal and should error.

This is more flexible. Let me design `parsePatternElement()`:
- If token is Ellipsis -> parse rest: `...` + parse pattern-or-expr? Rest target should be an identifier (per spec). Parse `parseIdent()` and wrap in
RestExpr.
- If token is `[` -> nested array pattern
- If token is `{` -> nested map pattern
- Else -> `parseExpr()` (which parses identifiers, binary ops, literals, calls, etc.)
- Then if current token is `=` (Assign), parse default expression and wrap as DefaultExpr. But wait: for map field `{x: a = 50}`, after parsing `a`,
token is `=`, we wrap. For array `[a = 50]`, same.
  But there's a conflict: in `parseExpr`, the token `=` is not an operator, so `parseExpr` will stop before `=`. Good. So after `parseExpr()`, if
token == Assign, we parse the default with `p.parseExpr()` and create DefaultExpr.
- What about `a := b` inside a pattern? Not allowed; `:=` isn't consumed by parseExpr, so pattern parser stops. Not relevant.

Hmm but `parseExpr()` on `a` in `[a, b]` returns Ident. On `a + b` returns BinaryExpr. Good.

For map pattern fields:
- Shorthand: `{x}` -> field key "x", value nil (shorthand).
- Rename: `{x: a}` -> key "x", value pattern `a`.
- Default shorthand: `{x = 50}` -> key "x", value DefaultExpr{Ident x, default 50}.
- Rename with default: `{x: a = 50}` -> key "x", value DefaultExpr{Ident a, default 50}.
- Nested: `{x: [a, b]}` -> key "x", value ArrayPattern.
- Could also have `{"key": a}`? Existing MapElementLit allows string keys. Task says map patterns bind by key, including shorthand `{x}` and renaming
`{x: a}`. I'll support string keys too for consistency: key can be Ident or String literal.

Parsing map pattern field:
```
keyPos := p.pos
keyName := ""
if p.token == Ident { keyName = p.tokenLit }
else if p.token == String { unquote }
else { error }
p.next()
if p.token == Colon {
    p.next()
    value := p.parsePatternElement() // or parsePattern()
    return &MapPatternField{Key:keyName, KeyPos:keyPos, Value:value}
}
// shorthand
if p.token == Assign {
    p.next()
    defaultExpr := p.parseExpr()
    return &MapPatternField{Key:keyName, Value:&DefaultExpr{Expr:&Ident{Name:keyName}, Default:defaultExpr}}

```
But wait: for shorthand with default, after parsing key name `x`, token is `=`. We parse default expression. Good.

  - If `Value` is a pattern (Ident, ArrayPattern, MapPattern, DefaultExpr) -> convert.
- DefaultExpr in a literal context is invalid (since `=` isn't part of literal). But if someone writes `{a: b = 1}` as an expression statement,
currently invalid; we can error.

Wait, but how does the conversion path get triggered? We parse `{a: b}` at statement start as MapPattern, then see next token (semicolon/end) is not
assignment, so we convert to MapLit and continue as expression. If `{a: b = 1}` then conversion would hit a DefaultExpr and error. Good.

But there's a deeper issue: `parseExprList` with patterns needs to handle binary operators after the literal. `{a: b} + x` as an expression statement.
After converting to MapLit, we need to continue binary parsing. I'll implement that.

Now, what about map patterns with string keys `{"a": b}` — as literal, also valid. Conversion works.

What about array patterns with rest: `[a, ...rest]` — rest is not valid in literal; conversion errors if not assignment context. Fine.

What about array patterns with defaults: `[a = 1]` — not valid literal; conversion errors if not assignment context. Fine.

So conversion only needs to handle valid literal elements: Ident, nested Array/Map pattern converted recursively, and general expressions (BinaryExpr
etc.). For array elements, any Expr that isn't DefaultExpr/RestExpr can be kept as-is (since we parsed general expressions). For map fields, keep
key/value if value isn't pattern-only.

Now the parsing flow in `parseSimpleStmt`:

```

switch p.token {
case token.Assign, token.Define:
    // if any LHS is a pattern, this is destructuring
```

But `parseExprListPattern` needs to parse the LHS. Let's define:

```go
func (p *Parser) parseExprListPattern() (list []Expr) {
    list = append(list, p.parseExprPattern())
    for p.token == token.Comma {
        p.next()
        list = append(list, p.parseExprPattern())
    }
    return
}

func (p *Parser) parseExprPattern() Expr {
    if p.token == token.LBrack || p.token == token.LBrace {
        pat := p.parsePattern()  // ArrayPattern or MapPattern
        // if next token is not assignment, convert to literal and continue binary
        if p.token != token.Assign && p.token != token.Define {
            lit := p.convertPatternToLiteral(pat)  // may return nil + error
            if lit != nil {
                return p.parseBinaryExprTail(lit, token.LowestPrec+1)
            }
            // if conversion failed (pattern-only in expression context), still return lit?
        }
        return pat
    }
    return p.parseExpr()
}
```

Wait, but `parseExpr()` itself starts with `parseBinaryExpr` which parses unary -> operand, and operand handles `[` and `{` as literals. If we call
`parseExpr()` for non-bracket tokens, fine.

For `parseBinaryExprTail`, we need to factor out the loop. Let me refactor `parseBinaryExpr`:

```go
func (p *Parser) parseBinaryExpr(prec1 int) Expr {
    x := p.parseUnaryExpr()
    return p.parseBinaryExprTail(x, prec1)
}

func (p *Parser) parseBinaryExprTail(x Expr, prec1 int) Expr {
    for {
        op, prec := p.token, p.token.Precedence()
        if prec < prec1 { return x }
        pos := p.expect(op)
        y := p.parseBinaryExpr(prec + 1)
        x = &BinaryExpr{...}
    }
}
```

Then `parseExprPattern` after converting to literal calls `parseBinaryExprTail(lit, token.LowestPrec+1)`.

But there's another issue: `parsePattern()` returns `Expr` but might also need to handle the case where the `[`/`{` is actually a literal that
contains invalid pattern syntax. Let's design `parsePattern()` to be lenient:

`parseArrayPattern`:

```
    elem := p.parsePatternElement()

    if !p.expectComma(RBrack, "array pattern element") { break }

rbrack := p.expect(RBrack)

```

```
    d := p.parseExpr()

```

Wait, for map fields, `parseMapPatternField` needs special handling for shorthand vs rename. For array, `parsePatternElement` is fine.

```
    p.next()

    return &MapPatternField{Key:keyName, KeyPos:keyPos, Value:value}
}
return &MapPatternField{Key:keyName, KeyPos:keyPos, Value:nil}
```

if value is `[b,c]` nested, `parsePatternElement` handles `[`. Good.

Now, conversion from pattern to literal:

```go
func (p *Parser) convertPatternToLiteral(pat Expr) Expr {
    switch pat := pat.(type) {
    case *ArrayPattern:
        var elems []Expr
        for _, e := range pat.Elements {
            switch e := e.(type) {
            case *RestExpr, *DefaultExpr:
                p.error(e.Pos(), "invalid expression")
                return nil
            default:
                elems = append(elems, p.convertPatternToLiteral(e))
            }
        }
        return &ArrayLit{Elements: elems, LBrack: pat.LBrack, RBrack: pat.RBrack}
    case *MapPattern:
        var elems []*MapElementLit
        for _, f := range pat.Fields {
            if f.Value == nil {
                p.error(f.KeyPos, "expected ':'")
                return nil
            }
            val := p.convertPatternToLiteral(f.Value)
            if val == nil { return nil }
            elems = append(elems, &MapElementLit{Key:f.Key, KeyPos:f.KeyPos, Value:val})
        }
        return &MapLit{LBrace: pat.LBrace, Elements: elems, RBrace: pat.RBrace}
    default:
        return pat
    }
}
```

But `convertPatternToLiteral` for `*Ident` returns ident as-is. For `*DefaultExpr` (invalid in literal), error. For `*RestExpr`, error. For
`*BinaryExpr` etc., return as-is.

Wait, `convertPatternToLiteral` is also called on nested patterns that could be `DefaultExpr` wrapping a nested pattern (e.g., `[a = [1,2]]` as a
default value). But `DefaultExpr` in a literal context is invalid, so error. Good.

Actually there's a subtle issue: when `parseExprPattern` parses `{a: b}` at statement start and converts to MapLit, then continues with binary tail.
Good. But what about a statement like `{a: b} := src`? It parses as MapPattern, sees `:=`, keeps pattern. Good.

Now, what about LHS with multiple patterns: `[a], [b] := ...`? `parseExprListPattern` would parse two patterns separated by comma; then `:=` with
multiple LHS. But compileAssign currently rejects numLHS > 1. For destructuring, should `[a], [b] := x, y` be allowed? The task says "Array patterns
bind by position. Map patterns bind by key... The same pattern forms are valid in function parameters." It doesn't mention multiple patterns in one
assignment. I think we can support single pattern (or possibly multiple patterns with multiple RHS like tuple assignment). To keep simple and match
likely tests, I'll support a single destructuring LHS with a single RHS, and error if multiple patterns. Actually `[a, b] := [1,2]` is a single
pattern with two bindings. Multiple LHS patterns would be `[a], [b] := ...` — unusual. I'll allow only one LHS when destructuring, consistent with
existing compileAssign (which allows only 1 LHS and 1 RHS for any assignment actually). Wait, compileAssign errors if numLHS > 1 or numRHS > 1: "tuple
assignment not allowed". So existing Tengo doesn't support multi-assignment at all. So destructuring is always single LHS, single RHS. Good, no extra
handling needed: compileAssign already errors if >1.

`parseIdentList` currently handles `...` before idents. I'll modify to parse each param via `parseParam()`:
```go
func (p *Parser) parseParam() Expr {
    if p.token == LBrack || p.token == LBrace {
        return p.parsePattern()
    }
    if p.token == token.Ellipsis {
        // handled by parseIdentList
    }
    x := p.parseIdent()
    if p.token == token.Assign {
        pos := p.expect(token.Assign)
        d := p.parseExpr()
        x = &DefaultExpr{Expr:x, Default:d, TokenPos:pos}
    }
    return x
}
```

But note: `parseIdentList`'s existing logic handles `...` and commas. I'll adapt:
```go
func (p *Parser) parseIdentList() *IdentList {
    lparen := p.expect(LParen)
    isVarArgs := false
    var params []Expr
    if p.token != RParen {
        if p.token == Ellipsis {
            isVarArgs = true
            p.next()
        }
        params = append(params, p.parseParam())
        for !isVarArgs && p.token == Comma {
            p.next()
            if p.token == Ellipsis {
                isVarArgs = true
                p.next()
            }
            params = append(params, p.parseParam())
        }
    }
    rparen := p.expect(RParen)
    return &IdentList{...}
}
```

`parseParam` should handle defaults on idents and patterns. But for a pattern param, can it have a default? e.g. `func([a] = [1]) {}` —
`parsePattern()` returns ArrayPattern, then token `=`; `parseParam` should check `=` after pattern too. So:
```go
func (p *Parser) parseParam() Expr {
    var x Expr
    if p.token == LBrack || p.token == LBrace {
        x = p.parsePattern()
    } else {
        x = p.parseIdent()
    }
    if p.token == Assign {
        pos := p.expect(Assign)
        d := p.parseExpr()
        x = &DefaultExpr{Expr:x, Default:d, TokenPos:pos}
    }
    return x
}
```

But there's a conflict: `parsePattern()` when parsing `[a]` as a param — it parses array pattern, and then if token is `=` it wraps. Good.

But `parsePattern` is lenient (parses general expressions too). In params, `func(a + b) {}` — parseParam calls parseIdent? No, token is Ident `a`, so
`x = parseIdent()`; then token is `+`, not `=`, so returns Ident `a`; then parseIdentList sees token `+` not comma/RParen, expects RParen -> error.
Current behavior also errors for `func(a + b)`. Good.

Now, `parseIdentList` is also used elsewhere? Only for func params (`parseFuncType`). And `IdentList` used in parser tests. So okay.

### Compiler changes

Now the meat. Let's design `compileDestructure`.

**Statement assignment:**

In `compileAssign`, before the existing logic, check if any LHS is a pattern (ArrayPattern/MapPattern/DefaultExpr/RestExpr) or if LHS is a simple
Ident with default (only in params). For assignment `:=`, if LHS is pattern, call `compileDestructure(node, lhs[0], rhs[0])`.

For `=` with pattern, error "cannot use destructuring with =".

Let's implement:
```go
func (c *Compiler) compileAssign(node parser.Node, lhs, rhs []parser.Expr, op token.Token) error {
    if len(lhs) == 1 && isDestructuringPattern(lhs[0]) {
        if op != token.Define {
            return c.errorf(node, "cannot use destructuring with =")
        }
        return c.compileDestructuringAssign(node, lhs[0], rhs[0])
    }
    ... existing ...
}
```

`isDestructuringPattern` returns true for ArrayPattern, MapPattern, DefaultExpr (top-level default only in params, not assignment), RestExpr.

`compileDestructuringAssign`:
```go
func (c *Compiler) compileDestructuringAssign(node parser.Node, pattern parser.Expr, rhs parser.Expr) error {
    // compile RHS into a temp local
    if err := c.Compile(rhs); err != nil { return err }
    temp := c.symbolTable.Define(c.uniqueName(":destructure"))
    c.emit(node, parser.OpDefineLocal, temp.Index)
    return c.compilePatternBindings(node, pattern, temp, nil)  // source is temp local
}
```

Wait, `temp` scope is local (the current scope). For top-level code (global scope), symbols are global by default? Let's check symbol_table.go.

package tengo

// SymbolScope represents a symbol scope.
type SymbolScope string

// List of symbol scopes
const (
	ScopeGlobal  SymbolScope = "GLOBAL"
	ScopeLocal   SymbolScope = "LOCAL"
	ScopeBuiltin SymbolScope = "BUILTIN"
	ScopeFree    SymbolScope = "FREE"
)

// Symbol represents a symbol in the symbol table.
type Symbol struct {
	Name          string
	Scope         SymbolScope
	Index         int
	LocalAssigned bool // if the local symbol is assigned at least once
}

// SymbolTable represents a symbol table.
type SymbolTable struct {
	parent         *SymbolTable
	block          bool
	store          map[string]*Symbol
	numDefinition  int
	maxDefinition  int
	freeSymbols    []*Symbol
	builtinSymbols []*Symbol
}

// NewSymbolTable creates a SymbolTable.
func NewSymbolTable() *SymbolTable {
	return &SymbolTable{
		store: make(map[string]*Symbol),
	}
}

// Define adds a new symbol in the current scope.
func (t *SymbolTable) Define(name string) *Symbol {
	symbol := &Symbol{Name: name, Index: t.nextIndex()}
	t.numDefinition++

	if t.Parent(true) == nil {
		symbol.Scope = ScopeGlobal

		// if symbol is defined in a block of global scope, symbol index must
		// be tracked at the root-level table instead.
		if p := t.parent; p != nil {
			for p.parent != nil {
				p = p.parent
			}
			t.numDefinition--
			p.numDefinition++
		}

	} else {
		symbol.Scope = ScopeLocal
	}
	t.store[name] = symbol
	t.updateMaxDefs(symbol.Index + 1)
	return symbol
}

// DefineBuiltin adds a symbol for builtin function.
func (t *SymbolTable) DefineBuiltin(index int, name string) *Symbol {
	if t.parent != nil {
		return t.parent.DefineBuiltin(index, name)
	}

	symbol := &Symbol{
		Name:  name,
		Index: index,
		Scope: ScopeBuiltin,
	}
	t.store[name] = symbol
	t.builtinSymbols = append(t.builtinSymbols, symbol)
	return symbol
}

// Resolve resolves a symbol with a given name.
func (t *SymbolTable) Resolve(
	name string,
	recur bool,
) (*Symbol, int, bool) {
	symbol, ok := t.store[name]
	if ok {
		// symbol can be used if
		if symbol.Scope != ScopeLocal || // it's not of local scope, OR,
			symbol.LocalAssigned || // it's assigned at least once, OR,
			recur { // it's defined in higher level
			return symbol, 0, true
		}
	}

	if t.parent == nil {
		return nil, 0, false
	}

	symbol, depth, ok := t.parent.Resolve(name, true)
	if !ok {
		return nil, 0, false
	}
	depth++

	// if symbol is defined in parent table and if it's not global/builtin
	// then it's free variable.
	if !t.block && depth > 0 &&
		symbol.Scope != ScopeGlobal &&
		symbol.Scope != ScopeBuiltin {
		return t.defineFree(symbol), depth, true
	}
	return symbol, depth, true
}

// Fork creates a new symbol table for a new scope.
func (t *SymbolTable) Fork(block bool) *SymbolTable {
	return &SymbolTable{
		store:  make(map[string]*Symbol),
		parent: t,
		block:  block,
	}
}

// Parent returns the outer scope of the current symbol table.
func (t *SymbolTable) Parent(skipBlock bool) *SymbolTable {
	if skipBlock && t.block {
		return t.parent.Parent(skipBlock)
	}
	return t.parent
}

// MaxSymbols returns the total number of symbols defined in the scope.
func (t *SymbolTable) MaxSymbols() int {
	return t.maxDefinition
}

// FreeSymbols returns free symbols for the scope.
func (t *SymbolTable) FreeSymbols() []*Symbol {
	return t.freeSymbols
}

// BuiltinSymbols returns builtin symbols for the scope.
func (t *SymbolTable) BuiltinSymbols() []*Symbol {
	if t.parent != nil {
		return t.parent.BuiltinSymbols()
	}
	return t.builtinSymbols
}

// Names returns the name of all the symbols.
func (t *SymbolTable) Names() []string {
	var names []string
	for name := range t.store {
		names = append(names, name)
	}
	return names
}

func (t *SymbolTable) nextIndex() int {
	if t.block {
		return t.parent.nextIndex() + t.numDefinition
	}
	return t.numDefinition
}

func (t *SymbolTable) updateMaxDefs(numDefs int) {
	if numDefs > t.maxDefinition {
		t.maxDefinition = numDefs
	}
	if t.block {
		t.parent.updateMaxDefs(numDefs)
	}
}

func (t *SymbolTable) defineFree(original *Symbol) *Symbol {
	// TODO: should we check duplicates?
	t.freeSymbols = append(t.freeSymbols, original)
	symbol := &Symbol{
		Name:  original.Name,
		Index: len(t.freeSymbols) - 1,
		Scope: ScopeFree,
	}
	t.store[original.Name] = symbol
	return symbol
}

	case *parser.IfStmt:
		// open new symbol table for the statement
		c.symbolTable = c.symbolTable.Fork(true)
		defer func() {
			c.symbolTable = c.symbolTable.Parent(false)

		if node.Init != nil {

		// first jump placeholder
		jumpPos1 := c.emit(node, parser.OpJumpFalsy, 0)
		if err := c.Compile(node.Body); err != nil {
			return err
		}
		if node.Else != nil {
			// second jump placeholder
			jumpPos2 := c.emit(node, parser.OpJump, 0)

			// update first jump offset
			curPos := len(c.currentInstructions())
			c.changeOperand(jumpPos1, curPos)
			if err := c.Compile(node.Else); err != nil {
				return err
			}

			// update second jump offset
			curPos = len(c.currentInstructions())
			c.changeOperand(jumpPos2, curPos)
		} else {
			// update first jump offset
			curPos := len(c.currentInstructions())
			c.changeOperand(jumpPos1, curPos)
		}
	case *parser.ForStmt:
		return c.compileForStmt(node)
	case *parser.ForInStmt:
		return c.compileForInStmt(node)
	case *parser.BranchStmt:
		if node.Token == token.Break {
			curLoop := c.currentLoop()
			if curLoop == nil {
				return c.errorf(node, "break not allowed outside loop")
			}
			pos := c.emit(node, parser.OpJump, 0)
			curLoop.Breaks = append(curLoop.Breaks, pos)
		} else if node.Token == token.Continue {
			curLoop := c.currentLoop()
			if curLoop == nil {
				return c.errorf(node, "continue not allowed outside loop")
			}
			pos := c.emit(node, parser.OpJump, 0)
			curLoop.Continues = append(curLoop.Continues, pos)
		} else {
			panic(fmt.Errorf("invalid branch statement: %s",
				node.Token.String()))
		}
	case *parser.BlockStmt:
		if len(node.Stmts) == 0 {
			return nil
		}

		c.symbolTable = c.symbolTable.Fork(true)
		defer func() {
			c.symbolTable = c.symbolTable.Parent(false)
		}()

		for _, stmt := range node.Stmts {
			if err := c.Compile(stmt); err != nil {
				return err
			}
		}
	case *parser.AssignStmt:
		err := c.compileAssign(node, node.LHS, node.RHS, node.Token)
		if err != nil {
			return err
		}
	case *parser.Ident:
		symbol, _, ok := c.symbolTable.Resolve(node.Name, false)
		if !ok {
			return c.errorf(node, "unresolved reference '%s'", node.Name)
		}

		switch symbol.Scope {
		case ScopeGlobal:
			c.emit(node, parser.OpGetGlobal, symbol.Index)
		case ScopeLocal:
			c.emit(node, parser.OpGetLocal, symbol.Index)
		case ScopeBuiltin:
			c.emit(node, parser.OpGetBuiltin, symbol.Index)
		case ScopeFree:
			c.emit(node, parser.OpGetFree, symbol.Index)
		}

func (c *Compiler) compileForInStmt(stmt *parser.ForInStmt) error {
	c.symbolTable = c.symbolTable.Fork(true)
	defer func() {
		c.symbolTable = c.symbolTable.Parent(false)
	}()

	// for-in statement is compiled like following:
	//
	//   for :it := iterator(iterable); :it.next();  {
	//     k, v := :it.get()  // DEFINE operator
	//
	//     ... body ...
	//   }
	//
	// ":it" is a local variable but it will not conflict with other user variables
	// because character ":" is not allowed in the variable names.

	// init
	//   :it = iterator(iterable)
	itSymbol := c.symbolTable.Define(":it")
	if err := c.Compile(stmt.Iterable); err != nil {
		return err
	}
	c.emit(stmt, parser.OpIteratorInit)
	if itSymbol.Scope == ScopeGlobal {
		c.emit(stmt, parser.OpSetGlobal, itSymbol.Index)
	} else {
		c.emit(stmt, parser.OpDefineLocal, itSymbol.Index)
	}

	// pre-condition position
	preCondPos := len(c.currentInstructions())

	// condition
	//  :it.HasMore()
	if itSymbol.Scope == ScopeGlobal {
		c.emit(stmt, parser.OpGetGlobal, itSymbol.Index)
	} else {
		c.emit(stmt, parser.OpGetLocal, itSymbol.Index)
	}
	c.emit(stmt, parser.OpIteratorNext)

	// condition jump position
	postCondPos := c.emit(stmt, parser.OpJumpFalsy, 0)

	// enter loop
	loop := c.enterLoop()

	// assign key variable
	if stmt.Key.Name != "_" {
		keySymbol := c.symbolTable.Define(stmt.Key.Name)
		if itSymbol.Scope == ScopeGlobal {
			c.emit(stmt, parser.OpGetGlobal, itSymbol.Index)
		} else {
			c.emit(stmt, parser.OpGetLocal, itSymbol.Index)
		}
		c.emit(stmt, parser.OpIteratorKey)
		if keySymbol.Scope == ScopeGlobal {
			c.emit(stmt, parser.OpSetGlobal, keySymbol.Index)
		} else {
			keySymbol.LocalAssigned = true
			c.emit(stmt, parser.OpDefineLocal, keySymbol.Index)
		}
	}

	// assign value variable
	if stmt.Value.Name != "_" {
		valueSymbol := c.symbolTable.Define(stmt.Value.Name)
		if itSymbol.Scope == ScopeGlobal {
			c.emit(stmt, parser.OpGetGlobal, itSymbol.Index)
		} else {
			c.emit(stmt, parser.OpGetLocal, itSymbol.Index)
		}
		c.emit(stmt, parser.OpIteratorValue)
		if valueSymbol.Scope == ScopeGlobal {
			c.emit(stmt, parser.OpSetGlobal, valueSymbol.Index)
		} else {
			valueSymbol.LocalAssigned = true
			c.emit(stmt, parser.OpDefineLocal, valueSymbol.Index)
		}
	}

	// body statement
	if err := c.Compile(stmt.Body); err != nil {
		c.leaveLoop()
		return err
	}

	c.leaveLoop()

	// post-body position
	postBodyPos := len(c.currentInstructions())

	// back to condition
	c.emit(stmt, parser.OpJump, preCondPos)

	// post-statement position
	postStmtPos := len(c.currentInstructions())
	c.changeOperand(postCondPos, postStmtPos)

	// update all break/continue jump positions
	for _, pos := range loop.Breaks {
		c.changeOperand(pos, postStmtPos)

Great, this gives a template for handling global vs local. I'll write helper methods:

```go
func (c *Compiler) emitGetSymbol(node parser.Node, sym *Symbol) {
    switch sym.Scope {
    case ScopeGlobal: c.emit(node, parser.OpGetGlobal, sym.Index)
    case ScopeLocal: c.emit(node, parser.OpGetLocal, sym.Index)
    case ScopeFree: c.emit(node, parser.OpGetFree, sym.Index)
    }
}

func (c *Compiler) emitDefineSymbol(node parser.Node, sym *Symbol) {
    switch sym.Scope {
    case ScopeGlobal: c.emit(node, parser.OpSetGlobal, sym.Index)
    case ScopeLocal:
        sym.LocalAssigned = true
        c.emit(node, parser.OpDefineLocal, sym.Index)
    case ScopeFree: ...
    }
}
```

Actually for destructuring in a local scope, leaf symbols are defined in the current symbol table and will be ScopeLocal. For top-level, ScopeGlobal.
So only Global/Local matter for destructuring.

But there's a subtlety with `:=` semantics: destructuring should always define new variables, so if a name already exists in the same block, `:=`
should error "redeclared in this block" (consistent with existing define). We should check that for each leaf identifier before defining. The existing
compileAssign for `:=` checks `if depth == 0 && exists` for the single ident. For destructuring, we should do the same for each leaf.

Also for function params, param names are defined in function scope. The current code:
```go
for _, p := range node.Type.Params.List {
    s := c.symbolTable.Define(p.Name)
    s.LocalAssigned = true
}
```
For pattern params, we need to define temps and leaf symbols differently.

Let me now design the compiler functions.

**Pattern binding compilation:**

```go
// compilePatternBindings compiles code to bind pattern to a source symbol (temp)
// sourceSymbol is the symbol of a variable holding the source value.
func (c *Compiler) compilePatternBindings(node parser.Node, pattern parser.Expr, source *Symbol) error {
    switch pat := pattern.(type) {
    case *parser.ArrayPattern:
        return c.compileArrayPattern(node, pat, source)
    case *parser.MapPattern:
        return c.compileMapPattern(node, pat, source)
    case *parser.Ident:
        // simple binding (used in params defaults? or direct leaf)
        return c.bindIdent(node, pat, source)
    case *parser.DefaultExpr:
        // default wrapping a pattern (used in params or nested? e.g. [a = 1])
        return c.compileDefaultPattern(node, pat, source)
    case *parser.RestExpr:
        return c.compileRestPattern(node, pat, source)
    default:
        return c.errorf(node, "invalid pattern element")
    }
}
```

Wait, but for array/map pattern elements, `compilePatternBindings` receives the source symbol which holds the *parent* value (e.g., the whole array).
To bind an element at index i:
1. Load source (temp).
2. Push index constant (Int/String).
3. OpIndex.
4. Define the binding.

For a simple leaf `a`:
```
GET source
CONST i
INDEX
DEFINE a
```
For a default `a = expr`:
```
GET source
CONST i
INDEX
JMPF defaultLabel   ; if undefined, pop, go to default
JMP endLabel
defaultLabel:
   <compile expr>
endLabel:
DEFINE a
```

For a nested pattern at element i (e.g., `[b,c]`):
```
GET source
CONST i
INDEX
DEFINE temp2        ; store subarray in temp2
<compile nested pattern bindings with source=temp2>
```

For rest `...rest` at position i:
```
GET source
CONST i          ; start index
CONST len(source)??
```
Hmm, we need the length of the source array. There's no direct "len" opcode. Options:
- Use slice operation: `source[i:]` — OpSliceIndex takes left, low, high (undefined for high). Compile source, push Int(i), push undefined/null,
OpSliceIndex, then define rest.
  But wait, OpSliceIndex expects high as `UndefinedValue` to mean end (not null!). Looking at VM code: `if high == UndefinedValue { highIdx =
numElements }`. And compiler's SliceExpr compiles `OpNull` for missing low/high. But VM's OpSliceIndex checks `if high == UndefinedValue`, not null!
Let me re-read: In VM:
  ```

      if low != UndefinedValue { ... }
      ...

          ...
          if high == UndefinedValue { highIdx = numElements } else if highInt, ok := high.(*Int) ...
  ```
  But compiler emits OpNull for missing bounds. OpNull pushes Null, which is not UndefinedValue. So how does `arr[:]` work? Let me check the Null

OpNull pushes UndefinedValue. Good, so `arr[:]` compiles as arr, undefined, undefined, OpSliceIndex.

For rest `...rest` at index i, compile:
```
GET source
CONST i      ; low
CONST undefined (OpNull)  ; high -> undefined -> end
SLICE
DEFINE rest
```
This creates a new array `source[i:]`. If i >= len, slice returns empty array (since highIdx defaults to numElements, and lowIdx clamped). Good.

But wait — rest should collect remaining array elements. If source is an ImmutableArray, OpSliceIndex returns *Array (new mutable array) — fine. If
source is a Map or other, OpSliceIndex would error "invalid slice index type" etc. That's a runtime error. Fine.

For a rest at top level of a function param, same.

Now, how to get the "index i" for each element? We can track the position index as an int constant. For array patterns, compile `Int{i}` constant for
element i. Note: positions are sequential 0..n-1. For rest, position is the index of the rest element.

For map patterns, compile key as `String{key}` constant. Use `OpIndex`.

Now, there's an important subtlety with defaults referencing earlier bindings: "Defaults may reference bindings established earlier in the same
operation." Since we process elements left-to-right and each binding is compiled/defined before later defaults, later defaults can reference earlier
variables because they're already defined in the symbol table and emitted as define. Good. E.g., `[a, b = a + 1] := [1]` -> b default references a,
which was defined first. Works.

But there's another subtlety: when a default expression references a binding that is later assigned in the same destructuring? Not required. Only
earlier.

Now, for map patterns, order of fields is source order (not map key order). Fine.

**Defining leaf identifiers:**

When we encounter an `*parser.Ident` as a pattern element (leaf), we need to:
- Check if it's `_`? In Tengo, `_` might be a valid identifier? In for-in, `_` is special (skipped). In destructuring, `_` could be a throwaway
binding? Not specified. `parseIdent` defaults name to "_" if not ident. I'll treat `_` as a normal binding (define a variable named `_`). Actually in
for-in, `_` is used to skip. For destructuring, it might be useful to allow `_` as throwaway. But not required; I'll just bind normally (same as any
ident). Actually if we bind `_`, then later uses of `_` would work. That's fine.

- Define symbol: `sym := c.symbolTable.Define(name)` (for `:=`). For destructuring in function params, the function scope. We should handle
redeclaration errors similarly.

But wait: in a destructuring assignment `[a, b] := src`, if `a` already exists in the same block, `:=` should error. The existing compileAssign does:
```go
symbol, depth, exists := c.symbolTable.Resolve(ident, false)
if op == token.Define {
    if depth == 0 && exists {
        return c.errorf(node, "'%s' redeclared in this block", ident)
    }
    ...
}
```
But `Resolve` with `recur=false` only resolves symbols in current table that are usable (LocalAssigned etc.). For a newly defined symbol in the same
block, `Resolve(name, false)` will find it if LocalAssigned (assigned at least once). But if the symbol is defined but not assigned (e.g., declared
earlier in the same block without assignment), `Resolve` may return false. Hmm. Let's look: `Resolve(name, recur)`:
```
symbol, ok := t.store[name]
if ok {
    if symbol.Scope != ScopeLocal || symbol.LocalAssigned || recur {
        return symbol, 0, true
    }
}
...
```
So for a local symbol in the same table that's not LocalAssigned, `Resolve(name,false)` returns not found, then searches parent. If parent has it,
returns parent symbol. So the redeclaration check `depth == 0 && exists` might miss local unassigned symbols. But in practice, symbols get
LocalAssigned=true once they're defined via `:=` or function params. The `define` case for `foo :=` marks... Let me check existing behavior: `x := 1;
x := 2` — does it error? Let's test.

func (c *Compiler) checkCyclicImports(
	node parser.Node,
	modulePath string,
) error {
	if c.modulePath == modulePath {
		return c.errorf(node, "cyclic module import: %s", modulePath)
	} else if c.parent != nil {
		return c.parent.checkCyclicImports(node, modulePath)
	}
	return nil
}

func (c *Compiler) compileModule(
	node parser.Node,
	modulePath string,
	src []byte,
	isFile bool,
) (*CompiledFunction, error) {
	if err := c.checkCyclicImports(node, modulePath); err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	compiledModule, exists := c.loadCompiledModule(modulePath)
	if exists {
		return compiledModule, nil
	}

	modFile := c.file.Set().AddFile(modulePath, -1, len(src))
	p := parser.NewParser(modFile, src, nil)
	file, err := p.ParseFile()
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	// inherit builtin functions
	symbolTable := NewSymbolTable()
	for _, sym := range c.symbolTable.BuiltinSymbols() {
		symbolTable.DefineBuiltin(sym.Index, sym.Name)
	}

	// no global scope for the module
	symbolTable = symbolTable.Fork(false)

	// compile module
	moduleCompiler := c.fork(modFile, modulePath, symbolTable, isFile)
	if err := moduleCompiler.Compile(file); err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	// code optimization
	moduleCompiler.optimizeFunc(node)
	compiledFunc := moduleCompiler.Bytecode().MainFunction
	compiledFunc.NumLocals = symbolTable.MaxSymbols()
	c.storeCompiledModule(modulePath, compiledFunc)
	return compiledFunc, nil
}

func (c *Compiler) loadCompiledModule(
	modulePath string,
) (mod *CompiledFunction, ok bool) {
	if c.parent != nil {
		return c.parent.loadCompiledModule(modulePath)
	}

OTHER STEP FIELDS

func (c *Compiler) currentInstructions() []byte {
	return c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].Instructions
}

func (c *Compiler) currentSourceMap() map[int]parser.Pos {
	return c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].SourceMap
}

func (c *Compiler) enterScope() {
	scope := compilationScope{
		SymbolInit: make(map[string]bool),
		SourceMap:  make(map[int]parser.Pos),
	}
	c.scopes = append(c.scopes, scope)
	c.scopeIndex++
	c.symbolTable = c.symbolTable.Fork(false)
	if c.trace != nil {
		c.printTrace("SCOPE", c.scopeIndex)
	}
}

func (c *Compiler) leaveScope() (
	instructions []byte,
	sourceMap map[int]parser.Pos,
) {
	instructions = c.currentInstructions()
	sourceMap = c.currentSourceMap()
	c.scopes = c.scopes[:len(c.scopes)-1]
	c.scopeIndex--
	c.symbolTable = c.symbolTable.Parent(true)
	if c.trace != nil {
		c.printTrace("SCOPL", c.scopeIndex)
	}
	return
}

func (c *Compiler) fork(
	file *parser.SourceFile,
	modulePath string,
	symbolTable *SymbolTable,
	isFile bool,
) *Compiler {
	child := NewCompiler(file, symbolTable, nil, c.modules, c.trace)
	child.modulePath = modulePath // module file path
	child.parent = c              // parent to set to current compiler
	child.allowFileImport = c.allowFileImport
	child.importDir = c.importDir
	child.importFileExt = c.importFileExt
	if isFile && c.importDir != "" {
		child.importDir = filepath.Dir(modulePath)
	}
	return child
}

func (c *Compiler) error(node parser.Node, err error) error {
	return &CompilerError{
		FileSet: c.file.Set(),
		Node:    node,
		Err:     err,
	}
}

Function scope uses `Fork(false)` (non-block), so Define creates ScopeLocal with indices starting from 0 in the function table. Good.

Now, for function params, the prologue needs to run before the body. Let's modify FuncLit compilation:

```go
c.enterScope()

// Define params
for i, p := range node.Type.Params.List {
    switch param := p.(type) {
    case *parser.Ident:
        s := c.symbolTable.Define(param.Name)
        s.LocalAssigned = true
    case *parser.DefaultExpr:
        // simple default param: define ident, mark assigned
        inner, ok := param.Expr.(*parser.Ident)
        if !ok { return error }
        s := c.symbolTable.Define(inner.Name)
        s.LocalAssigned = true
    case *parser.ArrayPattern, *parser.MapPattern, *parser.RestExpr:
        // define temp param symbol
        s := c.symbolTable.Define(fmt.Sprintf(":param%d", i))
        s.LocalAssigned = true
        // also need to track pattern for prologue
    default:
        return error
    }
}

// Compile prologue for destructuring params
for i, p := range node.Type.Params.List {
    if isPattern(p) or isDefault(p) {
        tempSym := resolve the :param symbol
        compile pattern bindings from tempSym
    }
}

c.Compile(node.Body)
```

But for a simple default param `a = 1`, we need to compile prologue: if arg is undefined, evaluate default and assign to local `a`. The source is the
arg slot itself, which is local index of `a`. So we can compile:
```
GETL aIdx
JMPF defaultLabel
JMP end
defaultLabel:
  compile default expr
end:
DEFL aIdx  (or SETL? it's already assigned)
```
Wait, but if arg is not undefined, we need to keep it. The sequence:
```
GETL aIdx       ; push arg
JMPF L_default  ; if undefined -> pop and jump
JMP L_end       ; else keep arg on stack and jump
L_default:
  <compile default>  ; push default
L_end:
DEFL aIdx
```
This redefines local a with either arg or default. Works. But note: `DEFL` vs `SETL`? Since a is a param marked LocalAssigned=true, we could use SETL.
But DEFL also works and is simpler. However, for closures/free variables, if the param is captured, the local slot might be an ObjectPtr? No, params
aren't pointers until captured. Let's use SETL to be safe? Actually SETL handles ObjectPtr update; DEFL just assigns the slot. In the prologue, before
body executes, no free vars exist yet, so no ObjectPtr. Either works. I'll use DEFL for consistency.

But there's a catch: the compiler needs to know the default expression may reference earlier params (e.g., `func(a, b = a + 1)`). Since a is defined
as a param symbol before b's default, and default compilation resolves `a`, good.

For pattern params, we define temp symbols for each pattern. The prologue for each pattern param reads from its temp local and binds leaves. E.g.,
`func([a,b], c) { }`:
- param 0: ArrayPattern -> define temp `:param0` at local 0, LocalAssigned=true.
- param 1: Ident c -> define `c` at local 1, LocalAssigned=true.
- prologue: compileArrayPattern with source `:param0`, which defines `a` (local 2) and `b` (local 3) via OpDefineLocal.
- NumLocals = 4.

But wait: `compileArrayPattern` needs to define leaf symbols via `c.symbolTable.Define`. The function scope symbol table already has `:param0` and
`c`. Define `a` -> local 2. Good.

For a default pattern param `[a] = [1]`, we'd define temp `:param0`, then prologue: check if source undefined; if undefined, evaluate default `[1]`
and store in temp, then destructure from temp. Let me handle DefaultExpr in params by compiling:
```
GETL tempIdx
JMPF defaultLabel
JMP end
defaultLabel:
   compile default expr
end:
SETL/DEFL tempIdx
; then compile pattern bindings with source=temp
```
But careful: after `JMPF`, if arg is undefined, we pop it and jump to default label; the default expr pushes value; then at `end` we need to store
into temp. If arg is not undefined, we `JMP end` but the arg remains on stack (JMPF didn't pop). So at `end`, we have either default value (if jumped
from default) or arg value. Then `DEFL tempIdx` stores it. Then destructure. Good.

Now, how does the compiler know which symbol is the temp for a pattern param? We can store a mapping or recompute by name. Since `:paramN` names are
deterministic, we can resolve `fmt.Sprintf(":param%d", i)`. Or we can keep a list. Let me use deterministic names and resolve with
`c.symbolTable.Resolve(name, true)` (or `Define`? it's already defined). Better: in the param definition loop, collect `paramSymbols []*Symbol`
aligned with `List`, plus `patterns []parser.Expr`. For simple params, symbol is the ident; for pattern params, symbol is temp; for default params,
symbol is the ident (or temp if default wraps pattern).

Actually simpler: after defining all params, iterate `node.Type.Params.List` and for each pattern/default, find the corresponding temp symbol by name.
Since we know the index and name pattern, use `c.symbolTable.Resolve(fmt.Sprintf(":param%d", i), true)`. That's clean.

Let me define:
```go
func paramSymbolName(i int) string { return fmt.Sprintf(":param%d", i) }
```

For simple default param `a = 1`, we don't need a temp; the source is local `a` itself. We can get symbol by `Resolve(inner.Name)`.

Now, one more thing: `NumParameters` is `len(node.Type.Params.List)`. For a function with pattern params, each pattern counts as 1 parameter (the
source). That matches VM arg count. Good.

But what about the existing `parseIdentList` VarArgs? `func(a, ...rest)` — rest is top-level variadic param. `IdentList.List` includes Ident `rest`
and `VarArgs=true`. The last param must be Ident. In compiler, `NumParameters = len(List)`, `VarArgs = node.Type.Params.VarArgs`. For a pattern param
combined with variadic? e.g., `func([a], ...rest)` — should be allowed? The pattern param counts 1, variadic rest collects remaining args. That seems
reasonable and should work with our design. The compiler's param definition loop: `[a]` -> temp :param0, `rest` -> ident. Prologue destructures
:param0. Fine.

But wait: if the last param is a pattern and VarArgs=true, that would be `func(...[a])` which is invalid. parseIdentList sets isVarArgs only when it
sees `...` before a param; then it parses `parseParam()` which could parse `[a]` as a pattern. That would be weird. We should error "rest parameter
must be an identifier" or similar. But not required by spec. Let's handle: in parseIdentList, if isVarArgs and the param is not Ident, error. Actually
simpler: in compiler, if VarArgs and last param isn't Ident, error.

Now let's think about the VM and stack correctness for function prologue.

When a CompiledFunction is called, the VM sets:
```
v.curFrame.basePointer = v.sp - numArgs
v.curInsts = callee.Instructions
v.ip = -1
v.framesIndex++
v.sp = v.sp - numArgs + callee.NumLocals
```
Then on next loop iteration, ip becomes 0 and executes prologue instructions. At that point, args are at stack[basePointer..basePointer+numArgs-1].
The prologue's OpGetLocal uses `basePointer + localIndex`. Good.

But note: `v.sp = v.sp - numArgs + callee.NumLocals` — if NumLocals > numArgs, sp points beyond args, and the slots for locals beyond args are
uninitialized (nil). The prologue's OpDefineLocal for leaf locals writes to those slots. Good.

func (c *Compiler) errorf(
	node parser.Node,
	format string,
	args ...interface{},
) error {
	return &CompilerError{
		FileSet: c.file.Set(),
		Node:    node,
		Err:     fmt.Errorf(format, args...),
	}
}

func (c *Compiler) addConstant(o Object) int {
	if c.parent != nil {
		// module compilers will use their parent's constants array
		return c.parent.addConstant(o)
	}
	c.constants = append(c.constants, o)
	if c.trace != nil {
		c.printTrace(fmt.Sprintf("CONST %04d %s", len(c.constants)-1, o))
	}
	return len(c.constants) - 1
}

func (c *Compiler) addInstruction(b []byte) int {
	posNewIns := len(c.currentInstructions())
	c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].Instructions = append(
		c.currentInstructions(), b...)
	return posNewIns
}

func (c *Compiler) replaceInstruction(pos int, inst []byte) {
	copy(c.currentInstructions()[pos:], inst)
	if c.trace != nil {
		c.printTrace(fmt.Sprintf("REPLC %s",
			FormatInstructions(
				c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].Instructions[pos:], pos)[0]))
	}
}

func (c *Compiler) changeOperand(opPos int, operand ...int) {
	op := c.currentInstructions()[opPos]
	inst := MakeInstruction(op, operand...)
	c.replaceInstruction(opPos, inst)
}

// optimizeFunc performs some code-level optimization for the current function
// instructions. It also removes unreachable (dead code) instructions and adds
// "returns" instruction if needed.
func (c *Compiler) optimizeFunc(node parser.Node) {
	// any instructions between RETURN and the function end
	// or instructions between RETURN and jump target position
	// are considered as unreachable.

	// pass 1. identify all jump destinations
	dsts := make(map[int]bool)
	iterateInstructions(c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].Instructions,
		func(pos int, opcode parser.Opcode, operands []int) bool {
			switch opcode {
			case parser.OpJump, parser.OpJumpFalsy,
				parser.OpAndJump, parser.OpOrJump:
				dsts[operands[0]] = true
			}
			return true
		})

	// pass 2. eliminate dead code
	var newInsts []byte
	posMap := make(map[int]int) // old position to new position
	var dstIdx int
	var deadCode bool
	iterateInstructions(c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].Instructions,
		func(pos int, opcode parser.Opcode, operands []int) bool {
			switch {
			case dsts[pos]:
				dstIdx++
				deadCode = false
			case opcode == parser.OpReturn:
				if deadCode {
					return true
				}
				deadCode = true
			case deadCode:
				return true
			}
			posMap[pos] = len(newInsts)
			newInsts = append(newInsts,
				MakeInstruction(opcode, operands...)...)
			return true
		})

	// pass 3. update jump positions
	var lastOp parser.Opcode
	var appendReturn bool
	endPos := len(c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].Instructions)
	newEndPost := len(newInsts)

	iterateInstructions(newInsts,
		func(pos int, opcode parser.Opcode, operands []int) bool {

		func(pos int, opcode parser.Opcode, operands []int) bool {
			switch opcode {
			case parser.OpJump, parser.OpJumpFalsy, parser.OpAndJump,
				parser.OpOrJump:
				newDst, ok := posMap[operands[0]]
				if ok {
					copy(newInsts[pos:],
						MakeInstruction(opcode, newDst))
				} else if endPos == operands[0] {
					// there's a jump instruction that jumps to the end of
					// function compiler should append "return".
					copy(newInsts[pos:],
						MakeInstruction(opcode, newEndPost))
					appendReturn = true
				} else {
					panic(fmt.Errorf("invalid jump position: %d", newDst))
				}
			}
			lastOp = opcode
			return true
		})
	if lastOp != parser.OpReturn {
		appendReturn = true
	}

	// pass 4. update source map
	newSourceMap := make(map[int]parser.Pos)
	for pos, srcPos := range c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].SourceMap {
		newPos, ok := posMap[pos]
		if ok {
			newSourceMap[newPos] = srcPos
		}
	}
	c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].Instructions = newInsts
	c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].SourceMap = newSourceMap

	// append "return"
	if appendReturn {
		c.emit(node, parser.OpReturn, 0)
	}
}

func (c *Compiler) emit(
	node parser.Node,
	opcode parser.Opcode,
	operands ...int,
) int {
	filePos := parser.NoPos
	if node != nil {
		filePos = node.Pos()
	}

	inst := MakeInstruction(opcode, operands...)
	pos := c.addInstruction(inst)
	c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].SourceMap[pos] = filePos
	if c.trace != nil {
		c.printTrace(fmt.Sprintf("EMIT  %s",
			FormatInstructions(
				c.scopes[c.scopeIndex].Instructions[pos:], pos)[0]))
	}
	return pos
}

func (c *Compiler) printTrace(a ...interface{}) {
	const (
		dots = ". . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . "
		n    = len(dots)
	)

	i := 2 * c.indent
	for i > n {
		_, _ = fmt.Fprint(c.trace, dots)
		i -= n
	}
	_, _ = fmt.Fprint(c.trace, dots[0:i])
	_, _ = fmt.Fprintln(c.trace, a...)
}

Undefined is falsy. But OpJumpFalsy uses `IsFalsy()` — so undefined triggers default. Also note `0`, `false`, `""` are falsy too. If a source value is
`0` at a position, default would apply incorrectly! E.g., `[a = 50] := [0]` would bind a=50, not 0. That's wrong per spec: defaults apply only when
missing. We need to check specifically for `*Undefined`, not falsy.

So we need a way to jump only if undefined. Existing opcodes: OpJumpFalsy checks IsFalsy (which includes 0, "", false). We need either:
- A new opcode `OpJumpIfUndefined` / `OpJumpIfNotUndefined`.
- Or use `OpEqual` with undefined constant to test, then jump.

Option 2: We can compile:
```
; value on stack
OpConstant undefinedConst  ; push undefined
OpEqual  ; result: true if value == undefined, false otherwise
OpJumpFalsy skipDefault ; if not undefined, pop false and skip
; value... wait, OpEqual consumed both and pushed bool, losing the value!
```
We need to keep the value. Could duplicate it first, but there's no OpDup. Hmm.

We could use a different approach: check undefined using `OpEqual` with a temp:
- Push value, push undefined, OpEqual -> bool. But value is consumed.
- Need to keep value. We could store value in a temp first, then test:
```
GET source
CONST idx
INDEX
DEFL temp2       ; store value in temp2
GETL temp2
CONST undefined
EQL
JMPF skipDefault ; if not undefined, skip
GETL temp2       ; value is undefined, but we'll compile default instead
JMP end
skipDefault:
GETL temp2
JMP end
defaultLabel...
```
This is doable but uses an extra temp per default. Alternatively add an OpDup opcode. Adding an opcode is a bit invasive but manageable. Let me
consider.

Actually there's a simpler approach: compile the default check as:
```
GET source
CONST idx
INDEX
; now value on stack
; We want: if value is undefined, pop it and eval default; else keep.
```
Use `OpJumpFalsy`? No, because 0/false/"" should not trigger default. So we need undefined-specific check.

Let me add a new opcode `OpJumpIfUndefined` that jumps (popping the value) if top is `*Undefined`, and leaves value if not undefined. This is clean
and minimal.

Similarly for default params.

Let's add:
```go
OpJumpIfUndefined Opcode = iota ... // after OpJumpFalsy maybe
```
Update OpcodeNames and OpcodeOperands `{4}`.

VM:
```go
case parser.OpJumpIfUndefined:
    v.ip += 4
    v.sp--
    if _, ok := v.stack[v.sp].(*Undefined); ok {
        pos := ...
        v.ip = pos - 1
    }
```
Wait, on not-jump, we want the value to stay on stack. If we decrement sp then on not-jump the value is at stack[sp] and sp points to it — it stays.
On jump, it's popped. Same pattern as OpJumpFalsy. Good.

But wait, `UndefinedValue` is `*Undefined`, and Array.IndexGet returns `UndefinedValue` (same singleton). Map too. So type check works.

Alternatively, could use OpEqual with a "undefined" and OpJumpFalsy but we'd lose value. New opcode is cleaner.

Now let's also consider: what if source is `undefined` itself (e.g., `[a, b] := undefined`)? Then `source[0]` via Undefined.IndexGet returns
UndefinedValue, so a binds undefined (and default applies). That's reasonable.

Now, for rest slice on a non-array, runtime error. Fine.

Let me also think about global vs local for temps and leaves.

`compileDestructuringAssign` at top-level:
- `temp := c.symbolTable.Define(":destructure")` -> ScopeGlobal. Emit `OpSetGlobal temp.Index` (not OpDefineLocal) to store RHS.
- For each leaf, `Define` -> ScopeGlobal. Emit `OpSetGlobal leaf.Index`.
- For loading temp, emit `OpGetGlobal`.
- For nested temp, also global.

Within a function body (local scope), destructuring assignment `[a,b] := src` inside function:
- `c.symbolTable` is the function's local table. `Define(":destructure")` -> ScopeLocal. Emit OpDefineLocal. Leaves -> ScopeLocal. Good.
- But what if inside a block within function? e.g., `if x { [a,b] := src }`. symbolTable is forked with block=true. Define creates ScopeLocal with
index from parent. OpDefineLocal works. Leaves ScopeLocal. Good.
- What about inside a block at top-level (global block)? e.g., `if x { [a,b] := src }`. symbolTable is forked block=true, parent is root global table.
Define with block=true: `nextIndex()` returns parent.nextIndex()+numDefinition; symbol.Scope = ScopeLocal (because t.Parent(true) != nil? Let's check:
`Parent(true)` skips block. For a block table whose parent is root global, `Parent(true)` returns root global (non-nil), so symbol.Scope =
ScopeLocal). So in a top-level block, `:=` creates a local variable. That's existing behavior (e.g., `if true { x := 1 }; println(x)` errors
unresolved). So for destructuring in top-level block, leaves are ScopeLocal and temp is ScopeLocal. OpDefineLocal works. Good.

So my helpers:
```go
func (c *Compiler) storeSymbol(node parser.Node, sym *Symbol) {
    switch sym.Scope {
    case ScopeGlobal:
        c.emit(node, parser.OpSetGlobal, sym.Index)
    case ScopeLocal:
        sym.LocalAssigned = true
        c.emit(node, parser.OpDefineLocal, sym.Index)
    default:
        panic(...)
    }
}

func (c *Compiler) loadSymbol(node parser.Node, sym *Symbol) {
    switch sym.Scope {
    case ScopeGlobal:
        c.emit(node, parser.OpGetGlobal, sym.Index)
    case ScopeLocal:
        c.emit(node, parser.OpGetLocal, sym.Index)
    case ScopeFree:
        c.emit(node, parser.OpGetFree, sym.Index)
    }
}
```

Wait, for ScopeLocal in a non-function top-level block, OpDefineLocal works because VM's main function is treated as a CompiledFunction with a frame?
Let's check: the main script is compiled as a function too. The VM runs the main function with a frame, basePointer=0, NumLocals. So OpDefineLocal
works even at top-level for locals defined in blocks. Good.

But careful: for a destructuring at top-level (not in block), leaves are ScopeGlobal, and `Define` returns ScopeGlobal. We emit OpSetGlobal. Good. The
temp is also global. At top-level, the main function has no locals for these, they're globals array. Good.

Now, there's a subtlety with `:=` redeclaration check in global: existing compileAssign checks `if depth == 0 && exists` for Define. For global
symbol, Resolve returns depth 0, exists true -> error. We'll replicate.

```go
package parser

import "strings"

// ArrayPattern represents an array pattern in destructuring.
type ArrayPattern struct {
    LBrack   Pos
    Elements []Expr
    RBrack   Pos
}

func (e *ArrayPattern) Pos() Pos { return e.LBrack }

func (e *ArrayPattern) String() string { ... }

    LBrace Pos
    Fields []*MapPatternField
    RBrace Pos
}

type MapPatternField struct {
    Key    string
    KeyPos Pos
    Value  Expr // pattern; nil if shorthand
}

type DefaultExpr struct {
    Expr     Expr
    Default  Expr
    TokenPos Pos
}

// RestExpr represents a rest pattern element: ...expr
type RestExpr struct {
    Expr        Expr
    EllipsisPos Pos
}
```

package parser

import (
	"strings"
)

// ArrayPattern represents an array pattern used in destructuring bindings.
type ArrayPattern struct {
	LBrack   Pos
	Elements []Expr
	RBrack   Pos
}

func (e *ArrayPattern) exprNode() {}

// Pos returns the position of first character belonging to the node.
func (e *ArrayPattern) Pos() Pos {
	return e.LBrack
}

// End returns the position of first character immediately after the node.
func (e *ArrayPattern) End() Pos {
	return e.RBrack + 1
}

func (e *ArrayPattern) String() string {
	var elements []string
	for _, m := range e.Elements {
		elements = append(elements, m.String())
	}
	return "[" + strings.Join(elements, ", ") + "]"
}

// MapPatternField represents a field in a map pattern.
type MapPatternField struct {
	Key    string
	KeyPos Pos
	Value  Expr // pattern expression; nil for shorthand
}

// MapPattern represents a map pattern used in destructuring bindings.
type MapPattern struct {
	LBrace Pos
	Fields []*MapPatternField
	RBrace Pos
}

func (e *MapPattern) exprNode() {}

// Pos returns the position of first character belonging to the node.
func (e *MapPattern) Pos() Pos {
	return e.LBrace
}

// End returns the position of first character immediately after the node.
func (e *MapPattern) End() Pos {
	return e.RBrace + 1
}

func (e *MapPattern) String() string {
	var fields []string
	for _, f := range e.Fields {
		if f.Value == nil {
			fields = append(fields, f.Key)
		} else {
			fields = append(fields, f.Key+": "+f.Value.String())
		}
	}
	return "{" + strings.Join(fields, ", ") + "}"
}

// DefaultExpr represents a pattern element with a default value: pat = expr.
type DefaultExpr struct {
	Expr     Expr
	Default  Expr
	TokenPos Pos
}

func (e *DefaultExpr) exprNode() {}

// Pos returns the position of first character belonging to the node.
func (e *DefaultExpr) Pos() Pos {
	return e.Expr.Pos()
}

// End returns the position of first character immediately after the node.
func (e *DefaultExpr) End() Pos {
	return e.Default.End()
}

func (e *DefaultExpr) String() string {
	return e.Expr.String() + " = " + e.Default.String()
}

// RestExpr represents a rest pattern element: ...expr.
type RestExpr struct {
	Expr        Expr
	EllipsisPos Pos
}

func (e *RestExpr) exprNode() {}

// Pos returns the position of first character belonging to the node.
func (e *RestExpr) Pos() Pos {
	return e.EllipsisPos
}

// End returns the position of first character immediately after the node.
func (e *RestExpr) End() Pos {
	return e.Expr.End()
}

func (e *RestExpr) String() string {
	return "..." + e.Expr.String()
}

// IsPattern returns true if the expression is a destructuring pattern.
func IsPattern(expr Expr) bool {
	switch expr.(type) {
	case *ArrayPattern, *MapPattern, *DefaultExpr, *RestExpr:
		return true
	}
	return false
}
EOF

// IdentList represents a list of function parameters.
type IdentList struct {
	LParen  Pos
	VarArgs bool
	List    []Expr
	RParen  Pos
}

// Pos returns the position of first character belonging to the node.
func (n *IdentList) Pos() Pos {
	if n.LParen.IsValid() {
		return n.LParen
	}
	if len(n.List) > 0 {
		return n.List[0].Pos()
	}
	return NoPos
}

// End returns the position of first character immediately after the node.
func (n *IdentList) End() Pos {
	if n.RParen.IsValid() {
		return n.RParen + 1
	}
	if l := len(n.List); l > 0 {
		return n.List[l-1].End()
	}
	return NoPos
}

// NumFields returns the number of fields.
func (n *IdentList) NumFields() int {
	if n == nil {
		return 0
	}
	return len(n.List)
}

func (n *IdentList) String() string {
	var list []string
	for i, e := range n.List {
		if n.VarArgs && i == len(n.List)-1 {
			list = append(list, "..."+e.String())
		} else {
			list = append(list, e.String())
		}
	}
	return "(" + strings.Join(list, ", ") + ")"
}
